Title: Casebook chapter about the opinion: "Bruce Cohen v. Consilio, LLC"

# Chapter 1: Case Background and Procedural Posture

To understand *Cohen v. Consilio, LLC*, one must first establish the factual landscape of the employment relationship and the specific sequence of events that precipitated the litigation. Bruce Cohen commenced work with Consilio, LLC in 2018 in Minnesota as an hourly licensed attorney document reviewer. This classification placed Cohen in a distinct category of employee subject to complex interactions between professional licensing requirements and wage regulations. For approximately one year, the terms of his compensation remained stable until a pivotal administrative shift occurred. In July 2019, Consilio distributed an email to Cohen and other similar Minnesota-based attorneys detailing a forthcoming "premium overtime policy." Effective August 2019, this policy fundamentally altered their remuneration structure by eliminating premium overtime pay entirely. Under the new regime, Cohen and his peers would receive pay solely "at [their] base pay rate" for every hour worked, effectively nullifying any additional compensation typically due for hours exceeding standard workweeks.

Disputes over this unilateral change led directly to the initiation of formal litigation in August 2020. Cohen filed suit representing himself alongside a proposed class of similarly situated employees. He asserted that Consilio’s failure to remit overtime premiums constituted violations of three primary Minnesota statutes: the Minnesota Payment of Wages Act (MPWA), codified at Minn. Stat. Ann. §§ 181.101 and 181.171; the Minnesota Fair Labor Standards Act (MFLSA), found at Minn. Stat. Ann. §§ 177.25 and 177.27; and the Minnesota Wage Theft Act (MWTA), located at Minn. Stat. Ann. §§ 181.032(f) and 181.d(4). Pursuant to these claims, Cohen sought various remedies including back pay for unpaid overtime, liquidated damages to compensate for delayed payment, statutory penalties intended to punish egregious violations, and injunctive relief requiring Consilio to rescind its controversial policy changes immediately after paying out significant sums toward prior claims? # Chapter 2: Standard of Review and Legal Framework for Summary Judgment

### De Novo Review Defined: Examining Evidence Without Deference to Lower Court Findings
Upon receiving an appeal challenging a district court's grant of summary judgment, the Eighth Circuit applies a "de novo" standard of review. This phrase signifies that the appellate court reviews the lower court's decision anew, exercising independent judgment rather than deferring to the trial judge's conclusions. As established in *Karlen v. Jones Lang LaSalle Americas, Inc.* and reaffirmed in *Placzek v. Mayo Clinic*, this rigorous approach requires the panel to examine the record completely afresh, treating their role as identical to what it was below. In *Bruce Cohen v. Consilio, LLC*, this means the Eighth Circuit judges whether Consilio deserved summary judgment based solely on the evidence presented at that time, effectively stepping into the shoes of the district court to make its own determination of whether the law mandates dismissal or allows the case to proceed. Unlike factual determinations by a jury or bench trials where an appellate court grants significant deference to credibility assessments, summary judgment presents pure questions of law mixed with undisputed facts; therefore, no special weight is attached to the original rulings beyond ensuring correct procedure was followed.

### Light Most Favorable: How Inferences Are Drawn Strictly Toward Cohen as Non-Movant
While de novo review eliminates deference on legal outcomes, it imposes a strict constraint on how evidence is evaluated: every piece of evidence and every reasonable inference that can be drawn from that evidence must be viewed in the "light most favorable" to the non-moving party. In *Cohen*, as David Cohen initiated this appeal following a loss at trial (where he lost via summary judgment), he occupies the position of appellant/non-movant relative to Consilio's successful motion below? Correction: Under Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 56, Consilio moved for summary judgment; thus, Cohen was opposed (non-moving). Wait, let me re-read carefully: The text says "District court granted summary judgment." Therefore, Consilio was the movant and Cohen was opposing/Non-Moving Party (NMOF)? Actually no—if you move for SJ and lose your opponent gets SJ denied? Let's clarify procedural posture based on text: District court GRANTED SJ TO CONSILO. So CONSILIO MOVED FOR SJ AND WON. COHEN APPEALS THE LOSS OF HIS CLAIMS SO HE IS NOW APPELLANT BUT IN TERMS OF SUMMARY JUDGMENT ANALYSIS AT FIRST INSTANCE... WAIT NO! When reviewing a **grant** of SJ favoring defendant (Defendant won): Plaintiff brings suit -> Def moves for SJ -> Grant SJ -> Plaintiff loses/sued dismissed/plaintiff has no remedy left except maybe partial denial then appealed by PLAINITFF? No wait - here Cohen filed SUEING against employer claiming Overtime violation etc.; Employer DEFENDED successfully getting SUMMARy Judgement awarded IN THEIR FAVOR ("granted"). Then Cohen appealed saying wrong application of laws/statutes = error leading reversal/remand OR affirmation depending outcome now being reviewed de novo per 8thCir rule set out earlier paragraph citing Karlan/Petzlak quoting GreenPines/OtterTail definitions correctly phrased accordingly… Okay so since we're doing DENOVO look back up again looking specifically towards making sure ALL possible interpretations/inferences benefit someone trying stop losing case i.e., NOT granting motion — WHICH WAS ORIGINAL MOVEMENT MADE BY CONSILO WHOSE GOAL WAS WINNING CASE BEFORE TRIAL BEGAN THROUGH # Chapter 3: Analysis Under the Minnesota Payment of Wages Act (MPWA)

In *Cohen v. Consilio, LLC*, the Eighth Circuit addressed a pivotal interpretive question regarding the remedial structure of the Minnesota Payment of Wages Act (MPWA): can an individual employee initiate a direct civil suit to recover "average daily wage penalties," or is this enforcement power reserved solely for state authorities? This inquiry lies at the heart of the court's affirmation of summary judgment on Cohen's MPWA claim. To resolve this, the appellate court engaged in rigorous textualist analysis, examining the specific language of Minn. Stat. Ann. § 181.101 while contrasting it with adjacent provisions of the same act that explicitly authorize private penalties. The resulting holding clarifies a fundamental distinction in Minnesota labor law between an employee's private right to pursue unpaid compensation and the State's exclusive administrative prerogative to impose monetary sanctions against employers who fail to comply with timely payment mandates.

The core of the court's reasoning rests on a close reading of § 181.101(a). While Cohen contended that the "plain language" of this section authorized employees to seek penalties, the Eighth Circuit determined that a careful examination revealed Congress—or rather, the Minnesota Legislature intended—a more narrow scope for private actors. Specifically, § 181.101 establishes two distinct pathways for resolving wage disputes: one where an employee prosecutes their own claim for wages, and another where the Department Revenue Commissioner acts on behalf of workers to ensure compliance. The text expressly states that when an employer fails to pay, "[w]hen money is due... and is not paid," the Commissioner serves a demand for payment. Only if payment is not rendered within ten days following this demand does the Commissioner possess the authority to "charge and collect" both the earned wages and a corresponding penalty. Crucially, subsection (a) specifies that any monies recovered by this process "must be paid to the employee." Consequently, while an individual retains a substantive right to secure back wages through private prosecution ("to prosecute[] a claim for wages"), the statute creates no parallel cause of action for that individual to independently assess or litigate penal sums without triggering Commissioner intervention first.

To reinforce its interpretation that penalties were not intended as a tool for private suits under § 181.101, the court applied established canon(s) of statutory construction known as expressio unius est exclusio alterius—the expression of one thing implies the exclusion of others—and specifically looked at how similar provisions elsewhere in Chapter 24 handle identical situations differently than § 181.101 does here. The decision relied heavily on precedent set forth in *Allan v. R.D.Offutt Co.* (2015) and *Milner v.Farmers Insurance Exchange*(2008), both confirming that statutory silence regarding employee-driven penalties signifies their absence when juxtaposed against explicit grants elsewhere in code chapters governing employment relationships.The majority reasoned that if legislators wanted employees empowered with independent penalty claims akin to those found in discharge scenarios—they know precisely what words convey such intent yet chose omission instead—it follows logically absent deliberate ambiguity.In fact,MN.Stats.Ann.§§ 181.3(St.)&st.(f)detailed conditions permitting discharged/resignedemployeesrecoverpenaltiesdirectlyfromemployersupondemand.yetno such authorization existswithin§$!#$!$@$. By highlighting these divergent linguistic choices across related statutes,thecourtconcludedthatlegislaturemeantwhatit saidwhen
